=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums of length n and an integer array queries.
Let gcdPairs denote an array obtained by calculating the GCD of all possible pairs (nums[i], nums[j]), where 0 <= i < j < n, and then sorting these values in ascending order.
For each query queries[i], you need to find the element at index queries[i] in gcdPairs.
Return an integer array answer, where answer[i] is the value at gcdPairs[queries[i]] for each query.
The term gcd(a, b) denotes the greatest common divisor of a and b.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [2,3,4], queries = [0,2,2]
Output: [1,2,2]
Explanation:
gcdPairs = [gcd(nums[0], nums[1]), gcd(nums[0], nums[2]), gcd(nums[1], nums[2])] = [1, 2, 1].
After sorting in ascending order, gcdPairs = [1, 1, 2].
So, the answer is [gcdPairs[queries[0]], gcdPairs[queries[1]], gcdPairs[queries[2]]] = [1, 2, 2].

Example 2:

Input: nums = [4,4,2,1], queries = [5,3,1,0]
Output: [4,2,1,1]
Explanation:
gcdPairs sorted in ascending order is [1, 1, 1, 2, 2, 4].

Example 3:

Input: nums = [2,2], queries = [0,0]
Output: [2,2]
Explanation:
gcdPairs = [2].

 
Constraints:

2 <= n == nums.length <= 10^5
1 <= nums[i] <= 5 * 10^4
1 <= queries.length <= 10^5
0 <= queries[i] < n * (n - 1) / 2

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def gcdValues(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[int]) -> List[int]:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Now, final answer code:
```
from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left

class Solution:
    def gcdValues(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[int]) -> List[int]:
        max_val = max(nums)
        freq = [0] * (max_val + 1)
        for x in nums:
            freq[x] += 1

        cnt = [0] * (max_val + 1)
        for d in range(1, max_val + 1):
            s = 0
            for m in range(d, max_val + 1, d):
                s += freq[m]
            cnt[d] = s * (s - 1) // 2

        for d in range(max_val, 0, -1):
            for m in range(2 * d, max_val + 1, d):
                cnt[d] -= cnt[m]

        for d in range(1, max_val + 1):
            cnt[d] += cnt[d - 1]

        return [bisect_left(cnt, q + 1) for q in queries]
```
Need think: In descending loop, when d = max_val, inner loop range(2*max_val, max_val+1, max_val) empty. Good.
But wait, in descending loop, `cnt[d]` initially pairs count. For d, we subtract `cnt[m]` for m multiple of d. But `cnt[m]` after descending is exact[m] only if m > d. Since m starts at 2d, yes. But what about if m = 2d and 2d > max_val? no loop. Good.
Potential issue: `cnt[d]` for d where d is not in any pair? pairs count 0, subtract exact multiples maybe 0, remains 0. Good.

Let's consider if there is an alternative issue: The exact gcd count for d should count pairs whose gcd is exactly d. Our formula `exact[d] = P[d] - sum_{m multiple of d, m>d} exact[m]`. Is `P[d]` equal to sum over all m multiple of d of exact[m]? Let's verify: A pair with gcd exactly g is counted in P[d] iff d divides g. Because if d divides both numbers iff d divides gcd? Actually if d divides both numbers, then d divides gcd. Conversely if d divides gcd, d divides both. So yes, P[d] counts all pairs whose gcd is a multiple of d. Thus P[d] = sum_{g: d|g} exact[g]. Good. Descending formula works.
